I’m overjoyed and you get entirely free dental care when pregnant! Oh but no dentists will take me, oh dear, better be careful. Fast forward a bit more, my gums are bleeding profusely every time I go near them with a brush. I phoned multiple dentists again, no one would take me but I was told to get a special super-soft brush and use medicated (but alcohol free mouth wash) so that’s what I did. I then get diagnosed with gestational diabetes and struggle with that for the next 5 months, unfortunately absolutely no-one told me how common periodontal disease is with GDM. I’m not seeing a dentist so I continue my normal routine and hope for the best!
> 
> My daughter is born, she’s beautiful and healthy and then 36hrs later my Granda (the only father I ever had) dies. He was diagnosed with terminal cancer at the beginning of my pregnancy, I spend the entire pregnancy utterly terrified my due date is a countdown to his death and that will ultimately mean I won’t be with him at the end - that’s exactly what happens. Cue crippling post partum depression, during the next 6 months the only energy I have is for my daughter. I stop looking after myself (this includes my teeth) almost entirely. I don’t have the mental capacity to think about my teeth, I spend almost every minute I’m not actively holding my daughter staring at the wall or sobbing. Eventually I end up being told by a psychiatrist I needed to give up on breastfeeding and go on medication, I made it 6 months and I’m told I should be proud of myself given the circumstances. It takes me another 8-9 months to start feeling like myself again. I was also diagnosed with OCD at this time and that has largely been manifesting as health anxiety, that is now including my teeth.
> 
> Now that the fog of war is fading and I’m starting to care about myself again I am horrified at the state of my teeth. I am so deeply embarrassed. I have gum recession on multiple teeth, I have a chunk out of one of my teeth, I have staining, there’s gaps that weren’t there before, I’m disgusted with myself. I’ve been looking after my teeth religiously since I came out of it and I finally found a dentist to take me, my first appointment isn’t until November so I’m just powering through and trying my best not to make things worse. I can’t put into words how much anxiety I’m experiencing about this looming dental appointment. I’m convinced I’m going to lose half my teeth, the dentist is going to lecture me for being so lazy, I’m never going to be able to leave the house again, I know the poor dentist is going to have to deal with me crying over this. I’m aware logically that I’m going to need some considerable work done but I think the realistic worse case scenario is losing the one tooth that’s been missing a chunk for the last year. My anxiety will not stop telling me I’m going to need dentures. I’ve tried so hard in that year to find dental care and I’ve been rejected up until now, I have really tried since I got my brain back, I know a huge part of this is simply my own fault and I’m just mortified. I don’t want my daughter to grow up being embarrassed by how her mother looks, that’s the prevailing worry even if it’s silly.

### 21. Parents seek a sense of self, connection and memory as well as a workable routine.

Current findings placement: §§1, 6–7; memory/photo and household rows; proposed identity subsection.

Assessment: **Extends**. Current conclusion (synthesis/findings.md): “The reader has a specific job, not a universal deficit”

What should change: Add wanted time with the baby, loss of hobbies or intimacy, bodily and sexual identity, alienation from caregivers, impossible-to-use fragments of free time, and grief over not remembering infancy. Some want to vent or be recognized rather than receive a plan. Positive or quiet unstructured parenting and peer reassurance are counterexamples to a universal activity deficit. The photograph job can be recognition of a lost period rather than producing a polished keepsake.

### 22. Pregnancy symptoms and uncertain findings change everyday capacity and expectations.

Current findings placement: §§1–3, 7, 9, 13; prenatal-orientation row.

Assessment: **Extends**. Current conclusion (synthesis/findings.md): “Parents often already own information, equipment or support.”

What should change: Acknowledge the stage before newborn routines: disabling nausea, food aversion, pain, insomnia, movement/test uncertainty, medication fears and feeling unable to complain after a wanted pregnancy. Online normalization can make a parent doubt serious loss of functioning; reassurance or a normal prior appointment may not answer the present question. Symptom absence can be as alarming as symptoms. These are different information and support jobs, not a reason to infer clinical truth or one universal pregnant persona.

### 23. Reproductive decisions involve control, ambivalence, timing, money, family disclosure and uncertain tests.

Current findings placement: §§1, 7, 9, 13; no dedicated findings section.

Assessment: **Extends**. Current conclusion (synthesis/findings.md): ““New mother” alone does not identify the job or establish willingness to buy a report.”

### 25. Shopping decisions depend on actual bodies, transport, space, use duration and the ability to change or return products.

Current findings placement: §§1–5, 7, 9–11; gear rank 5, childproofing rank 7 and wardrobe row.

Assessment: **Extends**. Current conclusion (synthesis/findings.md): “Which models/components fit the space, transport and adults?”

What should change: Keep compatibility but add premature-baby clothing, one-handed cot operation, secondhand history and recall worries, inaccessible replacement stock, nursery aesthetics sacrificed to space, swaddle rejection, a broken/inconvenient setup and the cost of changing already-bought adaptors. Parenthood can expose equipment choices the parent did not know would be theirs. A source's recall, safety or legal claim is retained as attributed history, not independently checked guidance.

### 26. Outings require feasible care, travel and local conditions, while some families enjoy them easily.

Current findings placement: §§1–3, 7, 9, 11; first-trip and wardrobe rows.

Assessment: **Extends**. Current conclusion (synthesis/findings.md): “physical fitting, local capacity, live interaction and cleanup can be the actual missing service.”

What should change: Add transit-gate width, stroller weight and roughness, voltage, baggage weight, a taxi trip without buying for one use, heat or cold, infection fears and care access abroad. Parent and infant capacity can make a short errand unmanageable even when a packing list is complete; another family loves regular restaurants. A bought holiday can purchase relief from meals and washing rather than child-free time. Country-linked accounts identify concrete constraints, not a launch-market willingness-to-pay ranking.

It’s pretty much the most stressful thing ever and really makes me not want to leave the house with her unless it’s absolutely essential. ### 27. Persistent infant distress leaves parents deciding what is normal, what to try and when to seek care.

Current findings placement: §§1–2, 5, 7, 9, 13; no full infant-health interpretation section.

Assessment: **Extends**. Current conclusion (synthesis/findings.md): “The same country evidence weakens a universal “more content” solution:”

What should change: Retain the unresolved symptoms and competing interpretations: reflux, gas, vomiting, stool, rash, teeth, feeding refusal and unexplained crying. A healthy-looking baby or reassuring growth may not remove daily impairment, and the source can be worried about sleeping too much rather than too little. Professional encounters and trials that failed explain subsequent searches. Do not infer the cause from the sequence, treat an initial symptom question as a proven condition, or substitute one more commercial plan for the account.

> Around 4 months old (June 2025) he only weighed 9lbs 7oz. We got admitted to a children’s hospital for 7 days. He had blood work done, ultrasounds done to about every organ in his body and they tried multiple different formulas and medications. They diagnosed him with GERD and failure to thrive. We were sent home with him only gaining 2oz and with the instructions of feeding him 3oz of breast milk fortified with Enfamil AR for extra calories. No more medication or anything else. His pediatrician also told us to feed him purées once a day and we had a follow up with GI in August to see his weight gain. This feeding routine was an improvement but did not cure it. He still spit up after every feed and more than a normal amount. We had good days and bad days. He was gaining weight but slowly. Fast forward to August when we went back for the follow up. He weighed 12lbs 10oz at 6 months old. The GI doctor was not impressed with his weight gain and we were told to try an amino acid based formula for 2 weeks. Well to get him to drink this was near impossible but after a month of trying we succeeded. He has been on that completely for over 2 weeks. When he started the formula he weighed 13lbs he now weighs 13lbs 4oz at 8 months old. Also his spit up has not improved at all. It is the exact same as when he was on breast milk if not worse. Before he wouldn’t spit up while sleeping (I know, weird) and on this formula he does which scares me. Now they want to scope him and take samples of the lining of his GI tract. At this point, I’m wondering if it’s just something he needs to grow out of? Is he just a small baby? Are there any other parents who have dealt with having a baby not even on the percentile chart? Do I put him through anesthesia and a hospital stay for them to find nothing wrong other than GERD? But if I don’t do it and something is wrong I would hate myself. I just don’t know what to do. He is crawling, babbling, sitting up on his own, plays with the toys, has interests, etc… He is a very fussy baby and is extremely difficult though. (His dad had colic as a baby too and I had acid reflux as an infant as well but ours never got to the point of failure to thrive.) Any advice would help.

> A non-pharmaceutical approach that has worked fairly well for us is to tilt the crib just a little to raise his head. You can also get foam wedges designed to go under the mattress. The idea is that it helps to stop the reflux from coming up so far, and helps them to swallow it down easier without feeling like they can't breathe, which is scary and guaranteed to have them wake up crying. After our health visitor recommended it, we bought one for our son's crib and it does seem to work. For his pram, I put a thick lever arch file under the mattress, which does about the same thing.

### 28. Recovered promotions broaden formats and alternatives, without establishing independent purchases.

Current findings placement: §§4, 8, 11, 13, 16; seller-format comparisons.

Assessment: **Confirms and extends**. Current conclusion (synthesis/findings.md): “Provenance changes what an account can establish, never whether its words are kept.”

What should change: Add creator-described emergency PDFs, free regression guides, emailed curricula plus office hours, a caregiver chat answer book, minimalist/offline trackers, nutrient/freezer meal planners, career-return content, location tools, personal-learning audio, tarot and affiliate gear. Preserve zero sales, proposed prices, free access, recruitment, affiliate disclosures and uncertain independence. Parent-creator origin is supply evidence; a solicitation asking whether parents would pay is not willingness to pay. Separately retain explicit consumer requests so their language is not lost among maker pitches.

> I’m a working mom of 4 (2 HS teens two college kids).  I made a career comeback after a 7 year career gap (was a SAHM in the early years, but been back in the workforce for 10years now). I’m in the 9–5 life, I work in Tech Consulting. I also have been trying a side hustle / passion project as a content creator, helping moms overcome a career gap, based on my personal experience.  […] I kept seeing Reels about people making six figures from YouTube or Instagram “working 4 hours a week,” and I thought… could that be me instead of staying in my 9-5 life for  another 10 years? […] I’ll be honest — the flexibility of creating sounds amazing (working your own hours, chasing passion projects). But the stability of my 9–5 comeback has been a lifesaver when it comes to paying for braces, covering health insurance, saving for retirement, and the stability of just knowing the lights will stay on. […] So for me, the 9–5 is my base. The creator work I’m doing is my side hustle and passion project. Both have value, but the highlight reels on social media don’t show the trade-offs. […] Question for you:
> If you were or are returning to work after a career break to raise kids, would you choose the stability of a 9–5 or the flexibility of creator life?

## Outliers and counter-examples

These counter-examples qualify the main patterns. They do not establish prevalence, clinical effects or a universal opposite rule. The complete fields are repeated here so the contrary words remain directly available.

### Physical visitors are unwanted, but remote support is valued.

Reader provenance: Saved search-extract reply values remote emotional support while rejecting household visitors.

[glu01-0004]
> I did, however, reach out to a lot of my friends and family that have been through it and made lots of texts and phone calls. They really helped with the post partum hormones and problems. They offered advice or just listened to me rant. That was my village! But a physical village, no way.

Reader provenance: Saved search extract: expectant parent fears that accepting help will create obligation and anxiety.

Reader provenance: Search-index reply reports sleep and mental-health tradeoff; supply change is an individual claim.

[glu03-0130]

I wanted to enjoy my time and I just held my breath and hoped my supply wouldn’t completely tank. Well, it did. It’s basically non-existent at this point, less than a half an oz every 2-3 hours kind of non-existent. Although I was fully aware that this was the risk I was taking I’d be lying if I said I am anything short of heartbroken. EP was never something I WANTED to do (I don’t think many of us here do) but it has become such a huge part of my life and I really did reach a point where I didn’t mind it. Honestly I was proud of my determination to keep going even though I was never able to produce more than 2 bottles of milk a day for my baby. I was proud of my 8-10 oz a day as silly as that sounds.  […] Although I’ve considered quitting MANY times I really hate the way the end of this journey has unfolded for me. I’m trying to focus on the positives: the time I’ll get back to spend playing with my baby, possibly being able to lose all this baby weight, not stressing about water food intake/supplements, etc. But I feel extremely guilty that I won’t be able to provide milk for my baby through sick season and I’m angry with myself for prioritizing my time on vacation over keeping up with my supply.

[…] I woke up thinking about this because I was too tired to get up today (like I am many mornings), having done some toddler wakings. My husband got our 2 year old out of bed and did the morning routine and brought me coffee in bed. I stumbled down the stairs to kiss her goodbye. My mother also always got coffee in bed, and often didn't say goodbye to me in the mornings as she was sleeping in.  […] What will make a difference in my mothering? I have a degree of personal and financial independence my SAHM mother never knew.  I work, and am a scientist living off of the fruit of my brain. I model that for my daughter. When I window shop, I do it at thrift stores. We consume modestly. My own family went bankrupt due to my parent's poor spending and this impacted me a lot growing up.    […] But by and large, one of the biggest differences is the man I married. My own father was physically and emotionally abusive, undependable, and profoundly angry. I married a gentle, soft, terribly sweet man who suffers from sadness but rarely anger.  […] Sometimes I look in the mirror and see my mother, and look at my daughter and see myself, replaying the same scenes, but with a different soundtrack, and a different feeling all together. ### A changed birth plan can still feel safe and empowering.

> 11:00PM - eight hours after my waters broke and **still no contractions.** My OBGYN comes in for her on call shift and decides to come talk to me. I am immediately in tears and she’s incredibly compassionate. She tells me I still have time to get contractions, that I can keep trying and we’ll revisit the conversation at 2AM. […] 4:30AM - No contractions, dilation is stalled at a five. I ask to speak to the anesthesiologist. If I’m going on pitocin, I’m not doing it without an epidural. […] 5:30AM - speak to my OBGYN again as the anesthesiologist sets up for my epidural. I cry about losing my plan, she assures me that I’m making the right choice to protect me and baby from infection.  […] 1:00PM - I tell the nurse I can feel the contractions through the epidural. She reminds me I can push the button to increase the dosage, but I decline. I tell her I want to feel them, because I’m going to need them to push. She tells me that most epidural patients just get coached by their team, but she’s willing to let my self-direct my pushes if that’s what I want. Cervical check: 8cm […] 3:15PM - Cervical check. “Um, you pretty much don’t have a cervix and I can feel baby’s head. I’m going to go tell your doctor.” While she’s gone, I have two contractions and the overwhelming urge to push through both. When she returns, I tell her that I really wanted to push. “Oh, I didn’t mean to imply you couldn’t push. No, you do that if you want, this is going to take a while.” I push on the next contraction and her eyes widen. “Oh! Never mind, I’m going to rush your doctor along!”  […] Once doc got there, I pushed 30 minutes in my perfectly dark and silent room, no coaching, no interventions, just got to do the damn thing. Then baby boy got to rest on my chest for an hour while his nurses examined him. I felt so safe and empowered even though nothing went as planned.

[…] I feel like I am waiting for the other shoe to drop. I have a history of depression and anxiety, so we had created a network around me of medical profesionals and backup plans for what we would do *when* i break down. I am very aware that PPD and PPA can come later on, and I have been sad and I have cried and I have been overwhelmed, but all in all I am happier than I can ever remember being. At the same time I am terrified, because I was warned about the hormone drop, those first 2 weeks you WILL get baby blues. I am doing well being in the moment, I am fine with the idea of PPA or PPD showing up, but I am almost wondering why I am happy and part of me feels like this is wrong and I feel guilty because it’s not what’s supposed to happen.  […] I’ve been thinking that maybe having an awful pregnancy has made this first week easier, does anyone have a similar experience? Did the other shoe drop? Were you ever able to accept the happiness? Another worry I have is that the crash that will come will hit even harder BECAUSE I feel good now, and that terrifies me.

## What fits nowhere

The current findings touch several of these in scope cautions or scattered paragraphs, but have no developed section for the underlying job. These are proposed additions to the research account, not automatically new product recommendations. Selected full excerpts are above; the other cited IDs are retained in the pattern support lists.

### Pregnancy-complication education and care navigation

The prenatal row discusses orientation, courses and hospital systems, but there is no developed section for what miscarriage care, testing, referrals, interpretation of records or fragmented follow-up will actually involve. The request can be an explicitly local explanation of procedures rather than generic labor preparation. [glu11-0126] [glu11-0127] [glu01-0094] [glu01-0095] [glu01-0124] [glu01-0128] [glu01-0272] [glu03-0085] [glu03-0086] [glu03-0087] [glu11-0377].

### Postpartum injury, autonomy and sexual/bowel/bladder recovery

The current shortlist has no recovery contender or complete findings section for failed tear repairs, prolapse, incontinence, inability to sit or lift a toddler, pain during intimacy, and the wish to avoid repeating a birth. A cushion comparison and paying for continuity are concrete requests/spending within that context; they are not a report transaction. [glu03-0082] [glu11-0295] [glu11-0338] [glu11-0339] [glu11-0399] [glu11-0324] [glu11-0407] [glu11-0408] [glu03-0205].

### Death, bereavement and the decisions around a lost baby

Neither the operational shortlist nor generic emotional context contains the decision about seeing/holding a baby after loss, the inability to say goodbye, a leave return after stillbirth, or a pump-shopping sequence overtaken by both twins' deaths. An earlier practical request must be read with its later update. [glu11-0159] [glu01-0029] [glu01-0030] [glu01-0031] [glu01-0036] [glu01-0037] [glu03-0307] [glu01-0150].

### Unintended pregnancy, minors, abortion/continuation, contraception and paternity disclosure

These need a reproductive-decisions section rather than being forced into a new-mother routine. The minor parent's housing and overnight-care questions, the ancestry-test parentage disclosure and the consideration of IVF/PGD to avoid sex disappointment are different jobs. Anti-abortion advice, pro-choice replies, ambivalence and pressure remain visible as source statements. [glu01-0008] [glu01-0009] [glu01-0010] [glu01-0035] [glu01-0068] [glu01-0330] [glu03-0100] [glu03-0083] [glu11-0150] [glu11-0151] [glu11-0370] [glu11-0371].

### Violence, racism, financial control and caregiver trust

These extend beyond an uncooperative adult or a packing/handoff checklist. A parent may have no safe, affordable alternative to alleged discriminatory care; leaving a violent partner can remove the care arrangement; inaccessible account balances can prevent spending decisions; racial naming pressure may coexist with refusal of emergency transport. [glu01-0322] [glu01-0323] [glu11-0323] [glu03-0206] [glu11-0302] [glu11-0303] [glu11-0372] [glu11-0373] [glu11-0374].

### Parental identity, joy, memory and explaining a parent's history

No current section fully covers lost interests, unusable fragments of free time, not recognizing infancy in photos, or explaining old self-harm scars to a child without encouraging imitation. These are not automatically requests for activity suggestions or a polished memory product. [glu01-0263] [glu03-0315] [glu11-0396] [glu11-0122] [glu11-0394].

### Care and income across generations

The return/leave comparison assumes the baby is the main coordination problem. A father's memory-care bill, nanny turnover, default daughter expectations and preferring jointly funded care over brothers' wives doing unpaid work introduce a different care/financial job. [glu11-0344] [glu11-0345] [glu11-0346] [glu01-0240] [glu03-0253].

### Older-child and whole-household needs

Sibling car-seat kicking, a child opening gates, school-age calm reading tools, toileting, screen controls, naming and family outings remain ordinary evidence. A global mother-and-baby set can retain their wider parenting context without claiming all belong to the infant report shortlist. ### Promotional markets outside the present contenders

Pregnancy/anxiety tarot, a career-return course, an ultrasound photo generator, a playground directory and configurable learning podcasts show other marketed formats. They are creator/seller or uncertain promotional accounts, not independent report buyers. ## Money

Every identified financial record is indexed here and reproduced in full in its pattern above. The inventory deliberately includes purchases, rental, paid help, income and leave constraints, prices, quotes, contemplated purchases, willingness/refusal to pay, free alternatives, gifts/resale, insurance, discounts and commercial offers. An amount can be a hypothetical, creator price, expected bill or local historical claim; those are not completed purchases. Currency codes are not inferred from a location or symbol. No new explicit refund amount is in this share; the ledger identifies the earlier-refunded coach account when interpreting renewed search.

### Prices, payment distinctions and financially decisive accounts

- Historical retail advice: 10 points for every £1, 90p in points per purchase and 3-for-2 gifts; no current-terms verification. [glu01-0064]

- Researched estimates of 30K a year for an au pair and 50k a year for a nanny; unaffordable to this author, currencies unstated. [glu01-0074]

- $5000 is a hypothetical cord-blood spending dilemma, not a reported transaction or benefit. [glu01-0077]

- $50-off referral offered, stacking with sales sought; chair purchase still planned. [glu01-0106]

- Potential willingness to pay for V-Hive's simple home program; no amount or purchase reported. [glu01-0119]

- $6000 private midwife considered against a free homebirth program; explicit willingness, financial concern and no completed purchase. [glu01-0130]

### Complete financial record index

Each line is a reader note identifying the record, not a new quotation or independent transaction. Where one field contains several prices, bills or purchases, its full reproduction above retains all of them.

#### Paid sleep help can disappoint, be abandoned or prompt another purchase.

- [glu01-0245] — Parent reports buying imperfect sleep app while treating reflux; severe exhaustion contrasts with easier first child, not a confirmed psychiatric diagnosis.

- [glu01-0258] — Secondhand SNOO and generic resources give limited help; variable evening shifts make consistent bedtime impractical.

- [glu03-0166] — First-time mother fears losing work after daycare struggles; multiple named sleep methods failed and consulting feels unaffordable or futile.

- [glu03-0215] — Parent describes rage and persistent poor sleep despite books, doctors and hiring a nanny.

- [glu03-0309] — After a previously quoted refunded coach trial, parent is ready to try again but insists on a holistic approach.

- [glu03-0321] — Existing program schedule, expensive formula and medicines have not solved waking; parent questions what paid consultation could add.

- [glu03-0324] — Parent questions whether expensive consulting adds anything beyond encouragement, with daycare nap scheduling outside parental control.

- [glu03-0325] — Second-time parent considers a $39 weaning guide versus consultant and wonders whether night feeds remain necessary.

- [glu03-0326] — Parent regrets a consultant’s schedule and settling advice, reports worsened crib aversion and sees daycare variability as an unresolved constraint.

- [glu03-0331] — Same parent reports over 1k spent on a prior consultant without a solution and rejects wake-window/cue advice.

- [glu11-0270] — After seeking free government help, parent considers thousands for in-home consultant execution; contemplated spending, not purchase.

- [glu11-0361] — Parent reports paying a sleep coach who provided no help despite following cues, wake windows and bedtime routine.

- [glu11-0428] — Second-time parent pays for Huckleberry Premium, then describes obsessive window-following, resentment and isolation.

- [glu11-0435] — Purchased sleep book has not helped; short crib naps and physical settling burden persist despite long overnight sleep.

- [glu11-0436] — Parents bought and read a sleep book but cannot implement techniques when infant becomes frightened and hyperventilates.

#### Targets can turn ordinary variation into guilt, fear and extra work.

- [glu11-0191] — Supply drop continues despite new pump parts, fitted flanges and additional sessions; parent wants to avoid formula.

- [glu11-0248] — Previously successful sleep cues become distress signals; parent tries a swing and awaits sleep books.

- [glu11-0278] — Algorithmic cot-death content intensifies sleep anxiety; parent resists app-based monitors despite contemplating one for reassurance.

- [glu03-0327] — Changed feeding after sleep training and consultant-directed bottles undermine prior supply confidence despite buying a deep freezer.

- [glu11-0249] — Snoo and other attempts fail; absent partner, repeated waking and teething uncertainty lead to bedsharing and questions about later sleep.

- [glu11-0439] — After allergy management, parent spends four hours daily driving for naps; night training helps but crib-nap gains disappear.

- [glu03-0293] — Parent seeks donor milk after recurrent mastitis, exhausted freezer supply and formula intolerance; current income is constrained.

- [glu11-0254] — Parent can afford formula and equipment but needs a routine preserving sleep and personal functioning before work return.

- [glu11-0327] — Parent tries pain relief, examines gums, buys different bottles and considers moving a pediatric appointment earlier.

- [glu11-0380] — Bought bottle-aversion book feels difficult to implement with a very small baby and nanny handoff days before work return.

#### A village is chosen: visitors can relieve work, impose obligations or violate privacy and boundaries.

- [glu01-0057] — Pregnant parent’s advance paid-help arrangement changes because caregiver is also expecting.

- [glu01-0058] — Respondent describes paid hands-on help after disabling birth recovery.

- [glu01-0116] — First-time mother compares expected cultural postpartum support with mother’s work priorities, in-law cancer and exhausted partner leave.

- [glu01-0254] — Parent accepts unrequested lengthy visit while exhausted after birth, then describes extra obligations and emotional fallout.

- [glu01-0365] — Saved search-extract respondent experiences offered meals as a transaction for baby access; personal account, not independent full-thread capture.

- [glu03-0088] — Pregnant author describes financial dependence, hostile in-laws, dirty housing and carrying the household work.

- [glu03-0254] — Expectant author weighs a respectful visitor against shared-bathroom privacy, recovery, breastfeeding and pregnancy mental-health difficulties.

- [glu11-0134] — Parent questions unequal emotional and financial grandparent support across families with different circumstances.

- [glu11-0282] — Parent would rather manage alone than host visitors who socialize or return a crying baby; occasional food gifts complicate resentment.

- [glu11-0329] — Peer urges flexibility while asserting that finding care a chore means being a babysitter; attributed beliefs, not a parenting standard.

- [glu11-0366] — Persistent illness while working and absent social support leave parent with little gear and unwilling to request a shower or welcome visitors.

#### Unsafe care, violence, coercion and discrimination can determine whether a handoff is possible.

- [glu01-0242] — Reported inability to pay delays custody action; no legal advice or adjudication inferred.

- [glu03-0205] — Parent describes painful sex, bleeding, injury, two young children and household work while partner says he is suffering.

- [glu03-0206] — After leaving an abusive partner, parent can cover basic consumables but lacks gear, clothing and affordable childcare.

- [glu03-0287] — Affordable paid daycare’s first day brings no nap, a bleeding rash and absent notification; parent plans to withdraw.

- [glu11-0293] — Parent cannot ignore toddler hitting because peers and dogs are involved; redirection fails and a relevant book was purchased.

- [glu11-0302] — Reduced earnings and inaccessible joint-account information leave parent dependent on partner transfers and unable to plan spending.

- [glu11-0313] — Parent disputes daycare illness thresholds, inconsistent app logs and restricted classroom access, and plans alternative-provider tours.

- [glu11-0323] — Leaving an abusive partner removes childcare coverage; promised family help fails to materialize and parent links this to pressure to stay.

#### Emotional care needs usable access, processing capacity and relief from blame, including for fathers.

- [glu11-0356] — Working mother finally obtains therapy options but lacks a private home or work space for video appointments and capacity to search again.

#### Parents seek a sense of self, connection and memory as well as a workable routine.

## Requests

This inventory includes every identified outright product, plan, list or service request, and also related requests for care instructions, usable routines, provider access, practical support and decisions. It is deliberately broader than commercial demand: asking peers for a routine, reassurance, medication experience or permission to stop is not an offer to pay. Some are retrospective requests made to a clinician or relative. Reader notes describe the ask; the original words are reproduced above when selected, and the full record remains at the cited ID otherwise. Creator/uncertain promotional solicitations are separated below.

### Paid sleep help can disappoint, be abandoned or prompt another purchase.

- [glu01-0258] — Secondhand SNOO and generic resources give limited help; variable evening shifts make consistent bedtime impractical.

- [glu01-0328] — Mother reads sleep book and contacts specialists yet cannot resolve short naps and waking; fears development harm and leaving home.

- [glu03-0166] — First-time mother fears losing work after daycare struggles; multiple named sleep methods failed and consulting feels unaffordable or futile.

- [glu03-0308] — Parent seeks experienced individual help for a twenty-one-month-old with low sleep needs and reported parasomnias.

- [glu03-0309] — After a previously quoted refunded coach trial, parent is ready to try again but insists on a holistic approach.

- [glu03-0321] — Existing program schedule, expensive formula and medicines have not solved waking; parent questions what paid consultation could add.

- [glu03-0324] — Parent questions whether expensive consulting adds anything beyond encouragement, with daycare nap scheduling outside parental control.

- [glu03-0325] — Second-time parent considers a $39 weaning guide versus consultant and wonders whether night feeds remain necessary.

- [glu03-0326] — Parent regrets a consultant’s schedule and settling advice, reports worsened crib aversion and sees daycare variability as an unresolved constraint.

- [glu03-0331] — Same parent reports over 1k spent on a prior consultant without a solution and rejects wake-window/cue advice.

- [glu11-0270] — After seeking free government help, parent considers thousands for in-home consultant execution; contemplated spending, not purchase.

- [glu11-0361] — Parent reports paying a sleep coach who provided no help despite following cues, wake windows and bedtime routine.

- [glu11-0435] — Purchased sleep book has not helped; short crib naps and physical settling burden persist despite long overnight sleep.

- [glu11-0436] — Parents bought and read a sleep book but cannot implement techniques when infant becomes frightened and hyperventilates.

## Integration corrections and provenance

**Contradicts the current scope wording, not the commercial ranking:** the current unfilter note says, “No new source reading or new voice ids were added.” That described the earlier restoration. These three reread files now supply 1,136 assigned records with new IDs. The final merge should date the change, replace the stale restoration-only account and reconcile all four shares before updating total quote, language-bank, source-reading and unread counts. Do not simply add excerpts to distinct-source or person totals. The older statements that all stored records are included and that omitted bodies remain a backlog need the same new-wave qualification.

Important dependence/sequence examples: [glu01-0029] [glu01-0030] [glu01-0031] move from a pump request to both twins dying; [glu01-0363] [glu01-0364] are linked same-author sleep setup/use accounts; [glu03-0082] [glu11-0295] repeat a missed-tear/repair account; [glu01-0226] [glu11-0289] [glu11-0290] [glu01-0227] [glu11-0291] contain overlapping postpartum accounts; [glu01-0221] [glu11-0343] and [glu01-0270] [glu11-0325] are overlapping menstrual/contraception accounts. [glu11-0130] [glu11-0131] are explicitly near-duplicate birth-history material. Source ledgers also flag cross-post provenance; multiple IDs are not multiple independent endorsements.

Saved scope matters: [glu03-0046] [glu03-0047] [glu03-0306] [glu11-0091] [glu11-0092] include title-only or abbreviated saves. [glu01-0001] [glu01-0002] [glu01-0003] [glu01-0004] [glu01-0365] are search-extract evidence, not new full live-thread reads. [glu01-0355] does not verify unavailable indexed buyer comments. Historical legal, immunization, drug, cot, positioning and product-recall claims stay attributed even when they conflict; restoration does not authenticate them.

No change is proposed to owner-buyer evidence: the share supplies public/source accounts and promotions, not Mamivio customers, receipts, conversions, causal efficacy or a costed fulfillment dataset. The strongest new commercial distinctions remain explicit charge versus access, contemplation versus payment, live execution versus a document, and buyer testimony versus a creator asking whether anyone would buy.

## Verification

Strict verification checks every reproduced block against the quote field for its immediately preceding ID, with no spelling, punctuation, case, whitespace or quotation-mark normalization. Every block is an exact source substring. Every ordinary block equals the complete field. The one source field containing a literal nested Markdown quotation marker is displayed in three consecutive excerpts; recombining them with the original paragraph separators equals that complete field exactly. Every mentioned quote ID exists in this assigned share, and every share ID is mentioned. All financial-index records have full verbatim reproductions.

Coverage: quotes in the share: 1,136; quotes reproduced verbatim: 657 distinct quote IDs (691 blockquote occurrences); further IDs listed: 479 distinct IDs without a reproduced block; IDs not mentioned at all: 0.
